					<div id="contentSub"><div id="mw-content-subtitle"></div></div>
					
					
					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">King of East Anglia from about 855 until 869</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Edward_the_Martyr" title="Edward the Martyr">Edward the Martyr</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output Anglia">Oswald</a></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color: #e4dcf6;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em 0.2em"><div style="height: 4px; style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;841</span></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">20 November 869<br />East Anglia</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Edmund the Martyr</b> (also known as <b>St Edmund</b> or <b>Edmund of East Anglia</b>, died 20 November 869)<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup> was king of <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_East_Anglia" title="Kingdom of East Anglia">East Anglia</a> from about 855 until his death. 
</p><p>Few historical facts about Edmund are known, as the kingdom of East Anglia was devastated by the <a href="/wiki/Vikings" title="Vikings">Vikings</a>, who destroyed any contemporary evidence of his reign. Coins minted by Edmund indicate that he succeeded <a href="/wiki/%C3%86thelweard_of_East_Anglia" title="Æthelweard of East Anglia">Æthelweard of East Anglia</a>, as they shared the same <a href="/wiki/Moneyer" title="Moneyer">moneyers</a>. He is thought to have been of East Anglian origin, but 12th century writers produced fictitious accounts of his family, succession and his rule as king. Edmund's death was mentioned in the <i><a href="/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_Chronicle" title="Anglo-Saxon Chronicle">Anglo-Saxon Chronicle</a></i>, which relates that he was killed in 869 after the <a href="/wiki/Great_Heathen_Army" title="Great Heathen Army">Great Heathen Army</a> advanced into East Anglia. <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Medieval</a> versions of Edmund's life and martyrdom differ as to whether he died in battle fighting the Great Heathen Army, or if he met his death after being captured and then refusing the Viking leaders' demand that he renounce Christ. 
</p><p>A popular <a href="/wiki/Cult_(religious_practice)" title="Cult (religious practice)">cult</a> emerged after Edmund's death, and he was <a href="/wiki/Canonised" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonised">canonised</a> by the Church. A series of coins commemorating him was minted from around the time East Anglia was absorbed by the kingdom of <a href="/wiki/Wessex" title="Wessex">Wessex</a> in 918, and in about 986, the French monk <a href="/wiki/Abbo_of_Fleury" title="Abbo of Fleury">Abbo</a> wrote of his life and martyrdom. 
</p><p>During the 10th century, Edmund's remains were <a href="/wiki/Translation_(relic)" title="Translation (relic)">translated</a> from an unidentified location in East Anglia to Beodricesworth (modern <a href="/wiki/Bury_St_Edmunds" title="Bury St Edmunds">Bury St Edmunds</a>); they were temporarily moved to London for safekeeping in 1010. Edmund's cult flourished during the <a href="/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages" title="Early Middle Ages">Early</a> and <a href="/wiki/High_Middle_Ages" title="High Middle Ages">High</a> Middle Ages, and he and <a href="/wiki/Edward_the_Confessor" title="Edward the Confessor">Edward the Confessor</a> were regarded as the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saints</a> of <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_England" title="Kingdom of England">medieval England</a> until they were replaced by <a href="/wiki/Saint_George" title="Saint George">Saint George</a> in the 15th century. Medieval manuscripts and works of art relating to Edmund include Abbo's <i>Passio Sancti Eadmundi</i>, <a href="/wiki/John_Lydgate" title="John Lydgate">John Lydgate</a>'s 14th-century <i>Life</i>, the <a href="/wiki/Wilton_Diptych" title="Wilton Diptych">Wilton Diptych</a>, and a number of church <a href="/wiki/Mural" title="Mural">wall paintings</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="King_of_the_East_Angles">King of the East Angles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: King of the East Angles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Accession_and_rule">Accession and rule</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Accession and rule">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Williamson_p16_3.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Williamson_p16_3.svg/300px-Williamson_p16_3.svg.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="264" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Williamson_p16_3.svg/450px-Williamson_p16_3.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Williamson_p16_3.svg/600px-Williamson_p16_3.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="691" data-file-height="607" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Williamson_p16_3.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The kingdom of the East Angles</div></div></div>
<p>The existence of Edmund is known from coins minted by his <a href="/wiki/Moneyer" title="Moneyer">moneyers</a>, three of whom—Dudda, Eadmund, and Twicga—minted coins for Edmund's predecessor, <a href="/wiki/%C3%86thelweard_of_East_Anglia" title="Æthelweard of East Anglia">Æthelweard</a> which suggests that a smooth transition of power occurred.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201840_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201840-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> The earliest documentary reference to Edmund is in the 870 <a href="/wiki/Annal" class="mw-redirect" title="Annal">annal</a> of the <i><a href="/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_Chronicle" title="Anglo-Saxon Chronicle">Anglo-Saxon Chronicle</a></i> compiled 20 years after his death. According to the historian Susan Ridyard, Edmund was born <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;841</span> and acceded to the East Anglian throne in <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;855</span>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard198861_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard198861-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Nothing is known of his life or reign from contemporary written sources. The devastation in East Anglia that was caused by the <a href="/wiki/Vikings" title="Vikings">Vikings</a> destroyed all the <a href="/wiki/Charter" title="Charter">charters</a> that may have referred to Edmund.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYorke200220_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYorke200220-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Edmund cannot be placed within any ruling dynasty. The 10th century French monk <a href="/wiki/Abbo_of_Fleury" title="Abbo of Fleury">Abbo of Fleury</a> stated that Edmund was <i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">ex antiquorum Saxonum nobili prosapia oriundus</i></span></i>, which according to Ridyard "was probably Abbo's rather verbose way of saying he was descended from the ancient nobility of his race".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard1988217_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard1988217-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A variety of different coins were <a href="/wiki/Mint_(facility)" title="Mint (facility)">minted</a> by Edmund's moneyers during his reign.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The letters <i>AN</i>, standing for 'Anglia', appear on the coins of only Edmund and <a href="/wiki/%C3%86thelstan_of_East_Anglia" title="Æthelstan of East Anglia">Æthelstan</a>, another 9th century king of the East Angles; the letters appear on Edmund's coins as part of the phrase <i>+ EADMUND REX AN[GLORUM]</i> ("Edmund, King of the Angles").<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung20187_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung20187-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Edmund's later coins read <i>+ EADMUND REX</i> ("Edmund, King").<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung20187_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung20187-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986294_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986294-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Otherwise, no chronology for his coins has been confirmed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986588_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986588-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_burial">Death and burial</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Death and burial">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>For decades after the <a href="/wiki/Lindisfarne#Viking_raid_on_the_monastery_(793)" title="Lindisfarne">Viking raid on Lindisfarne in 793</a>, their attacks on England were mainly raids on isolated <a href="/wiki/Monastery" title="Monastery">monastic communities</a>. According to the <i><a href="/wiki/Annales_Bertiniani" title="Annales Bertiniani">Annales Bertiniani</a></i> and the <i>Anglo-Saxon Chronicle</i>, a larger-scale attack occurred in <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;844</span>. By the end of the decade the Vikings had started to over-winter in England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOliver2013168_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOliver2013168-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> A larger force still, known to the <i>Anglo-Saxon Chronicle</i> <a href="/wiki/Chronicle" title="Chronicle">chroniclers</a> as the <i><span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">mycel heathen here</i></span></i> (‘<a href="/wiki/Great_Heathen_Army" title="Great Heathen Army">Great Heathen Army</a>’), appeared in 865. Three thousand men in hundreds of ships arrived off the east coast of England, probably from bases in Ireland. The army's first winter was spent in East Anglia before they moved on, arriving in <a href="/wiki/Northumbria" title="Northumbria">Northumbria</a> by 866/867.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOliver2013169,_172_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOliver2013169,_172-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The Great Heathen Army attacked Mercia by the end of 867 and made peaceful terms with the Mercians; a year later the Vikings returned to East Anglia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOliver2013172_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOliver2013172-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The <i>Anglo-Saxon Chronicle</i>, which generally described few matters relating to the East Angles and their rulers, relates that "here the army rode across Mercia into East Anglia, and took winter-quarters at Thetford; and that winter King Edmund fought against them, and the Danish took the victory, and killed the king and conquered all that land".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESwanton199770_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESwanton199770-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;note 2&#93;</a></sup> Where Edmund was killed and whether he died in battle or was murdered by the Danes afterwards is not known.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButlerThomasBurns1997173_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButlerThomasBurns1997173-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> The Great Heathen Army went on to invade <a href="/wiki/Wessex" title="Wessex">Wessex</a> in late 870, where they were confronted by <a href="/wiki/%C3%86thelred_of_Wessex" class="mw-redirect" title="Æthelred of Wessex">Æthelred of Wessex</a> and his brother, the future <a href="/wiki/Alfred_the_Great" title="Alfred the Great">Alfred the Great</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYorke1995109_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYorke1995109-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard1988211_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard1988211-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Edmund was buried in a wooden chapel near to where he was killed. At a date generally assumed by historians to have been during the reign of <a href="/wiki/%C3%86thelstan" title="Æthelstan">Æthelstan</a>, who became king of the Anglo-Saxons in 924, Edmund's body was <a href="/wiki/Translation_(relic)" title="Translation (relic)">translated</a> from <span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">Haegelisdun</i></span>—the location of which has never been conclusively identified—to <i><span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">Beadoriceworth</i></span></i>, now modern <a href="/wiki/Bury_St_Edmunds" title="Bury St Edmunds">Bury St Edmunds</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201875_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201875-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard1988213_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard1988213-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In 925 Æthelstan founded a religious community to take care of Edmund's <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorial_coinage">Memorial coinage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Memorial coinage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:King_Edmund_coin_(British_Museum).jpg" class="image"><img alt="photograph of a St Edmund coin" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/King_Edmund_coin_%28British_Museum%29.jpg/220px-King_Edmund_coin_%28British_Museum%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="108" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/King_Edmund_coin_%28British_Museum%29.jpg/330px-King_Edmund_coin_%28British_Museum%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/King_Edmund_coin_%28British_Museum%29.jpg/440px-King_Edmund_coin_%28British_Museum%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1364" data-file-height="671" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:King_Edmund_coin_(British_Museum).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A St Edmund memorial <a href="/wiki/Penny" title="Penny">penny</a> (<a href="/wiki/British_Museum" title="British Museum">British Museum</a>)</div></div></div>
<p>Following the death of the Danish <a href="/wiki/Guthrum" title="Guthrum">Guthrum</a>, king of East Anglia, in around 890,<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;note 3&#93;</a></sup> the same moneyers who had minted his coins started to produce money in commemoration of Edmund.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201872_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201872-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The coins, whose design was based upon those produced during Edmund's reign, provide the earliest evidence that he was venerated as a saint.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201869,_72_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201869,_72-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986305_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986305-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> All the <a href="/wiki/Penny" title="Penny">pennies</a> and (more rarely) <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_halfpenny#Early_halfpennies" title="History of the halfpenny">half-pennies</a> that were produced read <i>SCE EADMVND REX</i>—'O St Edmund the king!'. Some of them have a <a href="/wiki/Legend_(numismatics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Legend (numismatics)">legend</a> that provides evidence that the Vikings experimented with their initial design.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986320_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986320-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The St Edmund memorial coins were minted in great quantities by a group of more than 70 moneyers, many of whom appear to have originated from continental Europe; over 1800 specimens were found when the <a href="/wiki/Cuerdale_Hoard" title="Cuerdale Hoard">Cuerdale Hoard</a> was discovered in <a href="/wiki/Lancashire" title="Lancashire">Lancashire</a> in 1840.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986319_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986319-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The coins were widely used within the <a href="/wiki/Danelaw" title="Danelaw">Danelaw</a>. They have mainly been found in the east of England, but the exact location of any of the mints they came from is not known with certainty, although scholars have assumed that they were made in East Anglia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986319–320_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriersonBlackburn1986319–320-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cult_at_Bury_St_Edmunds">Cult at Bury St Edmunds</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Cult at Bury St Edmunds">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
  <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Saint Edmund the Martyr</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Lydgate_praying_at_St_Edmund%27s_shrine.png" class="image"><img alt="medieval illustration of Edmund&#39;s shrine" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/John_Lydgate_praying_at_St_Edmund%27s_shrine.png/220px-John_Lydgate_praying_at_St_Edmund%27s_shrine.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="133" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/John_Lydgate_praying_at_St_Edmund%27s_shrine.png/330px-John_Lydgate_praying_at_St_Edmund%27s_shrine.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/John_Lydgate_praying_at_St_Edmund%27s_shrine.png/440px-John_Lydgate_praying_at_St_Edmund%27s_shrine.png 2x" data-file-width="537" data-file-height="325" /></a><div class="infobox-caption"><a href="/wiki/John_Lydgate" title="John Lydgate">John Lydgate</a> prays at the shrine of St Edmund, from a folio of <i>Lives of SS Edmund and Fremund</i> (<a href="/wiki/British_Library" title="British Library">British Library</a>)</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a></span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Bury_St_Edmunds" title="Bury St Edmunds">Bury St Edmunds</a>, destroyed during the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Monasteries" class="mw-redirect" title="Dissolution of the Monasteries">Dissolution of the Monasteries</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">20 November</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">An arrow or a sword, a hand-held <a href="/wiki/Globus_cruciger" title="Globus cruciger">orb</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sceptre#Christian_era" title="Sceptre">sceptre</a>, wolf</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Kings, <a href="/wiki/Pandemic" title="Pandemic">pandemics</a>, wolves, torture victims, protection from the <a href="/wiki/Plague_(disease)" title="Plague (disease)">plague</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Edmund's <a href="/wiki/Cult_(religious_practice)" title="Cult (religious practice)">cult</a> was promoted and flourished, but it declined, with the production of St Edmund coins ceasing after around 910. The saint did not reappear in <a href="/wiki/Liturgical_year" title="Liturgical year">liturgical calendars</a> from the 9th century until the appearance of Abbo of Fleury's <i>Passio Sancti Eadmundi</i> three centuries later.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGransden199282–83_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGransden199282–83-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In 1010, Edmund's remains were translated to London to protect them from the Vikings, where they were kept for three years before being returned to Bury.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The Danish king <a href="/wiki/Cnut_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Cnut the Great">Canute</a>, who ruled England from 1016,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELawson2013_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELawson2013-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> converted to Christianity and was instrumental in founding the abbey at Bury St Edmunds.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201889_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201889-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The new stone <a href="/wiki/Abbey" title="Abbey">abbey</a> church was completed in 1032, having possibly been commissioned by Canute in time to be consecrated on the 16th anniversary of the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Assandun" title="Battle of Assandun">Battle of Assandun</a>, which took place on 18 October 1016.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201890_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201890-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Edmund's shrine became one of the most famous and wealthy <a href="/wiki/Pilgrimage" title="Pilgrimage">pilgrimage</a> locations in England. The abbey's power grew upon being given jurisdiction over the western half of the county of <a href="/wiki/Suffolk" title="Suffolk">Suffolk</a> by the creation in 1044 of the <a href="/wiki/Liberty_of_Saint_Edmund" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberty of Saint Edmund">Liberty of Saint Edmund</a>, established by <a href="/wiki/Edward_the_Confessor" title="Edward the Confessor">Edward the Confessor</a>, and a larger church was built in 1095, into which Edmund's <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> were translated.<sup id="cite_ref-High_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-High-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;note 4&#93;</a></sup> After the <a href="/wiki/Norman_conquest_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Norman conquest of England">Norman conquest of England</a> in 1066, the <a href="/wiki/Abbot" title="Abbot">abbot</a> planned out over 300 new houses within a <a href="/wiki/Grid_plan" title="Grid plan">grid-iron pattern</a> at a location that was close to the abbey precincts, a development which caused the town to more than double in size.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECantor1982176_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECantor1982176-38">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWaller200098_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWaller200098-39">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John,_King_of_England" title="John, King of England">King John</a> is said to have given a great <a href="/wiki/Sapphire" title="Sapphire">sapphire</a> and a precious stone set in gold to the shrine, which he was permitted to keep upon the condition that it was returned to the abbey when he died.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYates1843part_II_p._40_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYates1843part_II_p._40-40">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Edmund's shrine was destroyed in 1539, during the <a href="/wiki/English_Reformation" title="English Reformation">English Reformation</a>. According to a letter (now in <a href="/wiki/British_Library" title="British Library">British Library</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Cotton_Library" class="mw-redirect" title="Cotton Library">Cotton Collection</a>), the shrine was defaced, and silver and gold to the value of over 5000 <a href="/wiki/Mark_(money)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mark (money)">marks</a> was taken away. The abbot and his monks were expelled and the abbey was dissolved.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPinner20151–2_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPinner20151–2-41">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cult_at_Toulouse">Cult at Toulouse</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Cult at Toulouse">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1664, a lawyer from the French city of <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a> publicized a claim that Edmund's remains had been taken from Bury by the future <a href="/wiki/Louis_VIII_of_France" title="Louis VIII of France">Louis VIII of France</a> following his defeat at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Lincoln_(1217)" title="Battle of Lincoln (1217)">Battle of Lincoln</a> in 1217.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201812–13_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201812–13-42">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> The relics had then been donated by Louis to the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St._Sernin,_Toulouse" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of St. Sernin, Toulouse">Basilica of Saint-Sernin, Toulouse</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGem2020_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGem2020-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The first record of this is a relic list for Saint-Sernin of around 1425, which included St Edmund among the church's relics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGem2020_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGem2020-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In 1644, after the city was saved from the <a href="/wiki/Plague_(disease)" title="Plague (disease)">plague</a> from 1628 to 1631, which the population ascribed to the intercession of a saint known to the church authorities as <i>Aymundus</i>, who they decided was Edmund. In gratitude for its deliverance, the city vowed to build a new <a href="/wiki/Reliquary" title="Reliquary">reliquary</a> for the saint's remains. Edmund's cult flourished there for over two centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung2018133_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2018133-44">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The reliquary, designed by <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chalette" title="Jean Chalette">Jean Chalette</a>, was silver and adorned with solid silver statues.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJulien1996_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJulien1996-45">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> In 1644, the relics were verified and catalogued for interment in the newly-completed shrine, by which time the cult's origins had been forgotten.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung2018133–134_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2018133–134-46">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Edmund's shrine was removed in 1794 during the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolution" title="French Revolution">French Revolution</a>. The saint's relics were restored to the Basilica of St. Sernin in 1845 and placed in a new reliquary.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung2018135–136_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2018135–136-47">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics_at_Arundel">Relics at Arundel</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Relics at Arundel">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Arundel_Castle_-West_Sussex,_England-23June2011_(2).jpg" class="image"><img alt="aerial photo of Arundel" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Arundel_Castle_-West_Sussex%2C_England-23June2011_%282%29.jpg/220px-Arundel_Castle_-West_Sussex%2C_England-23June2011_%282%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Arundel_Castle_-West_Sussex%2C_England-23June2011_%282%29.jpg/330px-Arundel_Castle_-West_Sussex%2C_England-23June2011_%282%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Arundel_Castle_-West_Sussex%2C_England-23June2011_%282%29.jpg/440px-Arundel_Castle_-West_Sussex%2C_England-23June2011_%282%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="667" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Arundel_Castle_-West_Sussex,_England-23June2011_(2).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Arundel_Castle" title="Arundel Castle">Arundel Castle</a></div></div></div>
<p>In 1901 the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Westminster" title="Archbishop of Westminster">Archbishop of Westminster</a>, <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Vaughan" title="Herbert Vaughan">Herbert Vaughan</a>, received "certain relics" from the Basilica of Saint-Sernin. The relics, believed at the time to be those of St Edmund, were intended for the high altar of London's <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Cathedral" title="Westminster Cathedral">Westminster Cathedral</a>, which was then under construction.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGem2020_43-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGem2020-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The acceptance of the relics required the intercession of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_XIII" title="Pope Leo XIII">Pope Leo XIII</a>, after an initial refusal by the church in France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoughton197078_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoughton197078-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Upon their arrival in England they were housed in the <a href="/wiki/Fitzalan_Chapel" title="Fitzalan Chapel">Fitzalan Chapel</a> at <a href="/wiki/Arundel_Castle" title="Arundel Castle">Arundel Castle</a> prior to their translation to Westminster. Although their validity had been confirmed in 1874, when two pieces were given to <a href="/wiki/Henry_Edward_Manning" title="Henry Edward Manning">Edward Manning, Archbishop of Westminster</a>, concerns were raised about the authenticity of the Arundel relics by <a href="/wiki/M._R._James" title="M. R. James">Montague James</a> and Charles Biggs in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i>. The relics remained at Arundel under the care of the <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Norfolk" title="Duke of Norfolk">Duke of Norfolk</a> while a historical commission was set up by Cardinal Vaughan and Archbishop Germain of Saint-Sernin. They remain as of 1993<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> at Arundel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGem202045_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGem202045-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In 1966 three teeth from the collection of relics from France were given to <a href="/wiki/Douai_Abbey" title="Douai Abbey">Douai Abbey</a> in <a href="/wiki/Berkshire" title="Berkshire">Berkshire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGem2020_43-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGem2020-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Commemoration_and_attributes">Commemoration and attributes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Commemoration and attributes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St.Edmunds_Monument_(geograph_2705742).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/St.Edmunds_Monument_%28geograph_2705742%29.jpg/220px-St.Edmunds_Monument_%28geograph_2705742%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/St.Edmunds_Monument_%28geograph_2705742%29.jpg/330px-St.Edmunds_Monument_%28geograph_2705742%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/St.Edmunds_Monument_%28geograph_2705742%29.jpg/440px-St.Edmunds_Monument_%28geograph_2705742%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2304" data-file-height="3072" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St.Edmunds_Monument_(geograph_2705742).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Monument in <a href="/wiki/Hoxne" title="Hoxne">Hoxne</a>, Suffolk, marking the location of an ancient oak tree, supposed to be the site of Edmund's death.</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">feast day</a> of Edmund, King and Martyr in the Catholic Church is 20 November.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung2018100_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2018100-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> He is also remembered in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a>, with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">Lesser Festival</a> on this day of the year.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Edmund's particular attributes are the arrow and the sword,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOlderr201220,_198_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlderr201220,_198-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> being an English king, his attributes include the <a href="/wiki/Globus_cruciger" title="Globus cruciger">orb</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sceptre#Christian_era" title="Sceptre">sceptre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOlderr2012177_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlderr2012177-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> According to the <i><a href="/wiki/Oxford_Dictionary_of_Saints" title="Oxford Dictionary of Saints">Oxford Dictionary of Saints</a></i>, his attribute can also be a wolf.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137_22-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A stone cross at <a href="/wiki/Hoxne" title="Hoxne">Hoxne</a> in Suffolk marks one supposed location of Edmund's death. The monument records that it was built on the site of an ancient oak tree which fell in 1848 and was found to have an arrow head embedded in its trunk.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Some fifty-five <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England_parish_church" title="Church of England parish church">Church of England parish churches</a> are dedicated to Edmund, perhaps the most notable being the Church of <a href="/wiki/St_Edmund,_King_and_Martyr" title="St Edmund, King and Martyr">St Edmund, King and Martyr</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lombard_Street,_London" title="Lombard Street, London">Lombard Street</a> in the <a href="/wiki/City_of_London" title="City of London">City of London</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Benedictine" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedictine">Benedictine</a> community of <a href="/wiki/Douai_Abbey" title="Douai Abbey">Douai Abbey</a> also has Edmund as its patron.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Medieval_hagiographies_and_legends">Medieval hagiographies and legends</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Medieval hagiographies and legends">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Passio_Sancti_Eadmundi"><i>Passio Sancti Eadmundi</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Passio Sancti Eadmundi">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><span class="anchor" id="Passio_Sancti_Eadmundi"></span>
In about 986, the monks of <a href="/wiki/Ramsey_Abbey" title="Ramsey Abbey">Ramsey Abbey</a> commissioned Abbo of Fleury to write Edmund's <i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la"><a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">passio</a></i></span></i>, or account of his martyrdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGransden199247_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGransden199247-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> According to Abbo, <a href="/wiki/Dunstan" title="Dunstan">St Dunstan</a>, Archbishop of Canterbury, was the source of the story of the martyrdom, which he had heard told long before, in the presence of Æthelstan, by an old man who swore an oath that he had been Edmund's sword-bearer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard198863_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard198863-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Abbo's version of events, the king refused to meet the Danes in battle, preferring to die a martyr's death. According to Ridyard, Edmund's martyrdom cannot be proven and the nature of his fate—whether he died fighting or was murdered after the battle—cannot be read from the <i>Anglo-Saxon Chronicle</i>. Ridyard notes that the story that Edmund had an armour-bearer implies that he would have been a warrior king who was prepared to fight the Vikings on the battlefield, but she acknowledges the possibility that such later accounts belong to "the realm of hagiographical fantasy".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard198866–67_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard198866–67-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1062260506">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:50%; ; background-color: Cornsilk;">
<div class="quotebox-title" style="background-color: BlanchedAlmond; color: SaddleBrown;">Edmund's death, according to Ælfric of Eynsham</div>
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>"King Edmund, against whom Ivar advanced, stood inside his hall, and mindful of the Saviour, threw out his weapons. He wanted to match the example of Christ, who forbade Peter to win the cruel Jews with weapons. Lo! the impious one then bound Edmund and insulted him ignominiously, and beat him with rods, and afterwards led the devout king to a firm living tree, and tied him there with strong bonds, and beat him with whips. In between the whip lashes, Edmund called out with true belief in the Saviour Christ. Because of his belief, because he called to Christ to aid him, the heathens became furiously angry. They then shot spears at him, as if it was a game, until he was entirely covered with their missiles, like the bristles of a hedgehog (just like St Sebastian was).
</p><p>When Ivar the impious pirate saw that the noble king would not forsake Christ, but with resolute faith called after Him, he ordered Edmund beheaded, and the heathens did so. While Edmund still called out to Christ, the heathen dragged the holy man to his death, and with one stroke struck off his head, and his soul journeyed happily to Christ."
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="left-aligned" style="">Ælfric of Eynsham, <i>Old English paraphrase of Abbo of Fleury, 'Passio Sancti Eadmundi' </i><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>Abbo named one of Edmund's killers as Hinguar, who can probably be identified with Ivarr inn beinlausi (Ivar the Boneless), son of <a href="/wiki/Ragnar_Lodbrok" title="Ragnar Lodbrok">Ragnar Lodbrok</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard198867_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard198867-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> After describing the horrific manner of Edmund's death, the <i><span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Passio</i></span></i> continued the story. His severed head was thrown into the wood. As Edmund's followers searched for him, calling out "Where are you, friend?" the head answered, <span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">Her, her, her</i></span> ("Here! Here! Here!") until at last they found it, clasped between a wolf's paws, protected from other animals and uneaten. The followers then recovered the head.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard1988212_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard1988212-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201855_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201855-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbo failed to date these events surrounding Edmund's translation to <span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">Beodericsworth</i></span>, although from his text it can be seen that he believed that the relics had been taken to Beodericsworth by the time that <a href="/wiki/Theodred_(bishop_of_London)" title="Theodred (bishop of London)">Theodred</a> became <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_London" title="Bishop of London">Bishop of London</a> in around 926.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard1988231_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard1988231-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Upon <a href="/wiki/Exhumation#Exhumation" class="mw-redirect" title="Exhumation">exhumation</a> of the body, a miracle was discovered. All the arrow wounds upon Edmund's undecayed corpse had healed and his head was reattached.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERidyard1988212_61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERidyard1988212-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The last recorded inspection of the body whilst at Bury St Edmunds was in 1198.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGem2020_43-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGem2020-43">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoughton197051_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoughton197051-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The resemblance between the deaths of <a href="/wiki/St_Sebastian" class="mw-redirect" title="St Sebastian">St Sebastian</a> and St Edmund was remarked upon by Abbo: both saints were attacked by <a href="/wiki/Archery" title="Archery">archers</a>, although only Edmund is supposed to have been decapitated. His death bears some resemblance to the fate suffered by other saints: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Denis_of_Paris" title="Saint Denis of Paris">St Denis</a> was <a href="/wiki/Whip" title="Whip">whipped</a> and beheaded and the body of <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Egypt" title="Mary of Egypt">Mary of Egypt</a> was said to have been guarded by a <a href="/wiki/Lion" title="Lion">lion</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGransden199287_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGransden199287-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> The English <a href="/wiki/Medieval_studies" title="Medieval studies">medievalist</a> <a href="/wiki/Antonia_Gransden" title="Antonia Gransden">Antonia Gransden</a> described Abbo's <i>Passio</i> as "little more than a hotch-potch of hagiographical commonplaces" and argues that Abbo's ignorance of what actually happened to Edmund would have led him to use aspects of the <i>Lives</i> of well-known saints such as Sebastian and Denis as models for his version of Edmund's martydom. Gransden acknowledged that there are some aspects of the story—such as the appearance of the wolf that guards Edmund's head—that do not have exact parallels elsewhere.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGransden199286–87_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGransden199286–87-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Miracles_of_St_Edmund"><i>Miracles of St Edmund</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Miracles of St Edmund">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Herman_the_Archdeacon" title="Herman the Archdeacon">Herman the Archdeacon</a>, who was an excellent Latinist, wrote another hagiography of Edmund, the <i>Miracles of St Edmund</i>, at the end of the eleventh century. His original text does not survive, but a shortened version is part of a book dating to around 1100 produced by Bury St Edmunds Abbey, which is composed of Abbo's hagiography, followed by Herman's. The hagiographer and musician, <a href="/wiki/Goscelin" title="Goscelin">Goscelin</a>, soon afterwards produced a revised version of Herman's <i>Miracles</i>, which was hostile to Herman personally.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELicence2014xci,_cix,_cxiv–cxxix_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELicence2014xci,_cix,_cxiv–cxxix-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Both versions are printed and translated by <a href="/wiki/Tom_Licence" title="Tom Licence">Tom Licence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELicence2014_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELicence2014-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_legends">Other legends</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Other legends">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:12th-century_painters_-_Life_of_St_Edmund_-_WGA15723.jpg" class="image"><img alt="page from an illuminated manuscript" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/12th-century_painters_-_Life_of_St_Edmund_-_WGA15723.jpg/220px-12th-century_painters_-_Life_of_St_Edmund_-_WGA15723.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="328" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/12th-century_painters_-_Life_of_St_Edmund_-_WGA15723.jpg/330px-12th-century_painters_-_Life_of_St_Edmund_-_WGA15723.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/12th-century_painters_-_Life_of_St_Edmund_-_WGA15723.jpg/440px-12th-century_painters_-_Life_of_St_Edmund_-_WGA15723.jpg 2x" data-file-width="750" data-file-height="1117" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:12th-century_painters_-_Life_of_St_Edmund_-_WGA15723.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A 12th-century depiction of Edmund's martyrdom (<a href="/wiki/Morgan_Library_%26_Museum" title="Morgan Library &amp; Museum">Morgan Library &amp; Museum</a>, New York)</div></div></div>
<p><i>De Infantia Sancti Edmundi</i>, a fictitious 12th-century hagiography of Edmund's early life by the English <a href="/wiki/Canon_(priest)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon (priest)">canon</a> <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_of_Wells" title="Geoffrey of Wells">Geoffrey of Wells</a>, represented him as the youngest son of 'Alcmund', a <a href="/wiki/Saxons" title="Saxons">Saxon</a> king of Germanic descent. 'Alcmund' may never have existed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips1913295_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips1913295-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Edmund's fictitious continental origins were later elaborated upon in the 15th century by the poet John Lydgate in his <i>The Lives of Saints Edmund and Fremund</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReimer2004169_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReimer2004169-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Lydgate spoke of his parentage, his birth at <a href="/wiki/Nuremberg" title="Nuremberg">Nuremberg</a>, his adoption by <a href="/wiki/Offa_of_Mercia" title="Offa of Mercia">Offa of Mercia</a>, his nomination as successor to the king and his landing at <a href="/wiki/Old_Hunstanton" title="Old Hunstanton">Hunstanton</a> on the North Norfolk coast to claim his kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReimer2004179_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReimer2004179-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Edmund was said to have been crowned by <a href="/wiki/Humbertus" title="Humbertus">Humbert, bishop of Elmham</a> on 25 December 855, at a location known as <i><span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">Burna</i></span></i>, possibly <a href="/wiki/Bures_St_Mary" title="Bures St Mary">Bures St Mary</a> in Suffolk. At that time <i><span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">Burna</i></span></i> functioned as the royal capital.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoughton197019_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoughton197019-72">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Biographical details of Edmund in the <i>Catholic Encyclopedia</i>, published in 1913, include that "he showed himself a model ruler from the first, anxious to treat all with equal justice, and closing his ears to flatterers and untrustworthy informers".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips1913295_69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips1913295-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> It was written that he withdrew for a year to his royal tower at Hunstanton and learned the whole <a href="/wiki/Psalter" title="Psalter">Psalter</a>, so that he could recite it from memory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoughton197016_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoughton197016-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Edmund may have been killed at <a href="/wiki/Hoxne" title="Hoxne">Hoxne</a>, in Suffolk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWarner1996219_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarner1996219-74">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> His martyrdom is mentioned in a charter that was written when the church and chapel at Hoxne were granted to Norwich Priory in 1101. Place-name evidence has been used to link the name of Hoxne with Haegelisdun, named by Abbo of Fleury as the site of Edmund's martyrdom, but this evidence is dismissed by the historian Peter Warner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWarner1996139,_141_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarner1996139,_141-75">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> The association of Edmund's cult with the village has continued into modern times.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;note 5&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Dernford" class="mw-redirect" title="Dernford">Dernford</a> in Cambridgeshire,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoughton197024_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoughton197024-77">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Bradfield_St_Clare" title="Bradfield St Clare">Bradfield St Clare</a><sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> (near Bury St Edmunds) are other possible sites for where Edmund was martyred.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;note 6&#93;</a></sup> In a preface to Lydgate's <i>Life</i>, in which Edmund's banner—depicting three crowns set on a blue background—is described,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrantzen200468–69_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrantzen200468–69-80">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> the crowns are said to represent Edmund's martyrdom, virginity and kingship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPreble1917123_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPreble1917123-81">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;note 7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the <a href="/wiki/Folklore_studies" title="Folklore studies">folklorist</a> <a href="/wiki/Jennifer_Westwood" title="Jennifer Westwood">Jennifer Westwood</a>, the ancient wooden church of <a href="/wiki/Greensted_Church" title="Greensted Church">St Andrew, Greensted-juxta-Ongar</a>, is said to have been a resting place for his body on the way to Bury St Edmunds in 1013.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWestwood1986152_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestwood1986152-84">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Patronages">Patronages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Patronages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Edmund is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of <a href="/wiki/Pandemics" class="mw-redirect" title="Pandemics">pandemics</a> as well as kings,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBall2003276_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2003276-85">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> the Roman Catholic <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_East_Anglia" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of East Anglia">diocese of East Anglia</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> and Douai Abbey.<sup id="cite_ref-dou_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dou-87">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> England did not ever have a single patron saint before the <a href="/wiki/Tudor_period" title="Tudor period">Tudor period</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201811,_154_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201811,_154-88">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> during the Middle Ages, several saints were considered to have a close association with England and to be nationally important: St Edmund; <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_I" title="Pope Gregory I">St Gregory the Great</a>; <a href="/wiki/Edward_the_Confessor" title="Edward the Confessor">St Edward the Confessor</a>; <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Becket" title="Thomas Becket">St Thomas Becket</a>; and <a href="/wiki/St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="St George">St George</a>. Of these saints, Edmund was the most consistently popular with English kings,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201811_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201811-89">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> although <a href="/wiki/Edward_III_of_England" title="Edward III of England">Edward III</a> raised the importance of George when he associated him with the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Order of the Garter</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECantor1982176_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECantor1982176-38">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In 2006, <a href="/wiki/BBC_Radio_Suffolk" title="BBC Radio Suffolk">BBC Radio Suffolk</a> radio presenter Mark Murphy and <a href="/wiki/David_Ruffley" title="David Ruffley">David Ruffley</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of Parliament">Member of Parliament</a> for <a href="/wiki/Bury_St_Edmunds_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Bury St Edmunds (UK Parliament constituency)">Bury St Edmunds</a>, failed in their campaign to reinstate Edmund as the patron saint of England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung2018153_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2018153-90">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;note 8&#93;</a></sup>
In 2013, <a href="/wiki/BBC_News" title="BBC News">BBC News</a> reported a new campaign launched by Murphy and the <a href="/wiki/Brewing" title="Brewing">brewer</a> <a href="/wiki/Greene_King" title="Greene King">Greene King</a>, which is based in Bury St Edmunds, to reinstate St Edmund as England's patron saint. Supporters of the campaign stated their hopes that a petition could be used to force Parliament to debate the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BBC13_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC13-94">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_art">In art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: In art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The veneration of Edmund throughout the centuries has created a legacy of noteworthy works of art. An illustrated copy of Abbo of Fleury's <i>Passio Sancti Eadmundi</i>, made at Bury St Edmunds in around 1130, is now kept at the <a href="/wiki/The_Morgan_Library_%26_Museum" class="mw-redirect" title="The Morgan Library &amp; Museum">Morgan Library</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137_22-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The copy of <a href="/wiki/John_Lydgate" title="John Lydgate">John Lydgate</a>'s 15th-century <i>Life</i>, written for <a href="/wiki/Henry_VI_of_England" title="Henry VI of England">Henry VI of England</a>, is held at the British Library.<sup id="cite_ref-H2278_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H2278-95">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Wilton_Diptych" title="Wilton Diptych">Wilton Diptych</a> was painted during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Richard_II_of_England" title="Richard II of England">Richard II of England</a> and is the most famous representation of Edmund in art. <a href="/wiki/Panel_painting" title="Panel painting">Painted on oak panels</a>, it shows Edmund and Edward the Confessor as the royal patrons of England presenting Richard to the <a href="/wiki/Madonna_(art)" title="Madonna (art)">Virgin and Child</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137_22-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarmer2011136–137-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> The poet John Lydgate (1370–1451), who lived all his life in Bury St Edmunds, presented his twelve-year-old king Henry VI of England with a long poem (now known as <i>Metrical Lives of Saints Edmund and Fremund</i>) when Henry came to the town in 1433 and stayed at the abbey for four months.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrantzen200466–67_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrantzen200466–67-97">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> The book is now kept by the British Library in London.<sup id="cite_ref-BL-2278_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BL-2278-82">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Edmund's martyrdom features on several medieval wall-paintings to be found in churches across England.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;note 9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-packed" style="text-align:left">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 111.33333333333px"><div style="width: 111.33333333333px">

			<div class="gallerytext">

</p>

		</div></li>
</ul>
<p>The saint features in a romantic poem, <i><a href="/wiki/Athelston" title="Athelston">Athelston</a></i>, whose 15th-century author is unknown. In the climactic scene of the poem, Edyff, the sister of King 'Athelston' of England, gives birth to Edmund after passing through a ritual <a href="/wiki/Ordeal_by_fire#Ordeal_of_fire" class="mw-redirect" title="Ordeal by fire">ordeal by fire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFieldBrewer2010140_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFieldBrewer2010140-99">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/List_of_Catholic_saints" title="List of Catholic saints">List of Catholic saints</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ragener" title="Ragener">Ragener</a> - reputedly a relative of St Edmund, who shared in his martyrdom.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edmund_the_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-2"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-2">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The year of Edmund's death may have been 870, according to some calculations. The uncertainty has arisen because the <i><a href="/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_Chronicle" title="Anglo-Saxon Chronicle">Anglo-Saxon Chronicle</a></i> sometimes began in September, meaning that an event that took place in November 869 (according to the modern calendar) would have been recorded by the Anglo-Saxons as having taken place in 870.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESwanton1997xv_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESwanton1997xv-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-16"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-16">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><i><span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">Her rad se here ofer Mierce innan East Engle and wiñt setl namon. æt Đeodforda. And þy wint' Eadmund cying him wiþ feaht. and þa Deniscan sige naman þone cyning ofslogon. and þæt lond all ge eodon.</i></span></i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEarle186572,_74_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEarle186572,_74-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-24"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-24">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Guthrum who ruled East Anglia under the <a href="/wiki/Christian_name" title="Christian name">baptismal name</a> of Æthelstan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECostambeys2008_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECostambeys2008-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-37"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-37">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The <a href="/wiki/Liberty_(division)" title="Liberty (division)">Liberty</a> remained a separate jurisdiction under the control of the abbot of <a href="/wiki/Bury_St_Edmunds_Abbey" title="Bury St Edmunds Abbey">Bury St Edmunds Abbey</a> until the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_monasteries" title="Dissolution of the monasteries">dissolution of the monasteries</a> in the 1530s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERedstone1914202_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERedstone1914202-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-76"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-76">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Until 1849, an old tree stood in Hoxne Park that was believed to be where Edmund had been martyred. In the heart of the tree, an arrowhead was found. A piece of the tree was used to form part of an altar of a church dedicated to Edmund. Another legend relates that after being routed in battle, Edmund hid under the Goldbrook bridge at Hoxne, but his hiding place was revealed to a wedding party, who gave him away to his enemies.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-79"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-79">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">However, there is a spot where places named in the early accounts occur close together. A field called 'Hellesdon' lay just south of Pitcher's Green at <a href="/wiki/Bradfield_St_Clare" title="Bradfield St Clare">Bradfield St Clare</a>; Sutton Hall stands a mile south of Bradfield St Clare on the parish boundary; Kingshall Farm, Kingshall Green and Kingshall Street occur in <a href="/wiki/Rushbrooke_with_Rougham" title="Rushbrooke with Rougham">Rougham</a>, two miles to the north. Bradfield St Clare is approximately six miles from Bury St Edmunds, which was an Anglo-Saxon royal vill (settlement). A monastery already existed, founded by King Sigeberht in 633AD. There was also a building called Bradfield Hall that stood within the St Edmund's Abbey, and accounts show that the Abbey's Cellarer paid rent for small pieces of land at Bradfield St Clare Hall (6 <a href="/wiki/Shilling_(British_coin)" title="Shilling (British coin)">shillings</a> 8d <a href="/wiki/Penny_(British_pre-decimal_coin)" title="Penny (British pre-decimal coin)">pence</a>) and Sutton Hall (3s 2d.).</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-83"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-83">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The three crowns banner (representing the <a href="/wiki/Coat_of_arms" title="Coat of arms">coat of arms</a> of Bury St Edmunds) appears in Lydgate's book.<sup id="cite_ref-BL-2278_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BL-2278-82">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-92"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-92">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Ruffley had taken up the cause and helped deliver a large petition to the government in <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a>. <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister">Prime Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Tony_Blair" title="Tony Blair">Tony Blair</a> rejected the request; however, their attempt was successful on another level: "St Edmund (was) named patron saint of Suffolk...the high point of a successful campaign which was launched by Breakfast show presenter Mark Murphy and producer Emily Fellows in the autumn of 2006".<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-98"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-98">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Churches with surviving wall paintings of Edmund can be found at <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.paintedchurch.org/subinx2.htm">Medieval Wall Painting in the English Parish Church</a> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20120612065324/http://www.paintedchurch.org/subinx2.htm">Archived</a> 12 June 2012 at the <a href="/wiki/Wayback_Machine" title="Wayback Machine">Wayback Machine</a> website: D to F.</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1134653256">.mw-parser-output .side-box{margin:4px 0;box-sizing:border-box;border:1px solid #aaa;font-size:88%;line-height:1.25em;background-color:#f9f9f9;display:flow-root}.mw-parser-output .side-box-abovebelow,.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{padding:0.25em 0.9em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-image{padding:2px 0 2px 0.9em;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-imageright{padding:2px 0.9em 2px 0;text-align:center}@media(min-width:500px){.mw-parser-output .side-box-flex{display:flex;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{flex:1}}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .side-box{width:238px}.mw-parser-output .side-box-right{clear:right;float:right;margin-left:1em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-left{margin-right:1em}}</style><div class="side-box side-box-right plainlinks sistersitebox"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1126788409">.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style>
